What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thanks for seeing me. Yeah, so, what brings me in... well, it’s been building up for a while now. I’m a medical services manager, so I oversee operations for a few different clinics, which means there’s always a lot of moving parts. I'm usually fine when I'm on my feet, you know, putting out fires and talking to the nursing staff or dealing with patient complaints. \n\nBut the main reason I’m here is that anything that takes real, sitting-down brain work... I just feel like I hit a wall. Like, a literal brick wall. For instance, I have to do these detailed monthly reports on our budget variances, or right now I'm supposed to be planning this big multi-site community health event, and I just keep putting it off. I keep pushing it to the bottom of the pile, even when I know it really, really matters. \n\nI know the deadlines are looming, I know my directors need those numbers, but the thought of just sitting at my desk and focusing on that kind of heavy mental task... my brain just completely rejects it. It’s incredibly frustrating because I’ve worked really hard to get to this level in my career, especially having started out with just my associate's degree and having to prove myself over the years, and now I just can't seem to force myself to do the most important parts of my job. I'll find a hundred other little things to do instead of just sitting down and doing the one big thing I actually need to do. It's just getting to be too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about doing those detailed reports or planning the health event?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, I wouldn't say it's anxiety. I mean, I know what anxiety feels like, my sister has terrible anxiety, and she gets the racing heart and the sweating and all that, and it's not like that for me at all. It's more just... a complete lack of ability to start. \n\nLike, when I think about the budget variance report, which is due on the fifth of every month, by the way, and requires me to pull data from three different scheduling systems and cross-reference it with our billing software... it's not that I'm scared of the report. I know how to do it. I've done it a dozen times. But when I look at the icon on my desktop, or when I open the Excel file, it's just this overwhelming feeling of... ugh. Just, ugh. My brain just slides right off it. \n\nI don't sit there worrying about whether I'll do a bad job or if the directors will be mad at me—well, I mean, I do worry about them being mad if it's late, but the actual task itself doesn't make me nervous. It's just so incredibly tedious and requires so much sustained attention, and I just find myself thinking, \"Oh, I should really check if the maintenance guy fixed that leaky faucet in clinic B,\" or \"I need to reply to that email from the vendor about the new gloves.\" And before I know it, two hours have passed, and I haven't typed a single number into the spreadsheet. So, no, not anxious. Just... completely unable to make myself do it, if that makes sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed when you're unable to start or complete these tasks?\n\nPatient: Sad or depressed? No, I really wouldn't use those words. I mean, sure, I get frustrated, like I was saying before, because I know I should just be able to do it, you know? But it's not a sadness. It's not like I'm sitting at my desk crying over the budget report or feeling hopeless about my life. I actually really like my life, and I like my job—most of it, anyway. \n\nI'm not walking around feeling down in the dumps or, I don't know, empty or anything like that. It's more of an annoyance with myself. Like, I'll be sitting there, staring at the screen, and I'll think, \"Come on, man, just do the thing.\" But my brain is just stubbornly refusing. And then I get mad at myself for wasting time, especially when I know I'm perfectly capable of doing the work. I mean, I didn't get to be a manager by being stupid, right? I started out at the front desk with just my associate's degree and worked my way up, so I know how to work hard. \n\nBut this specific kind of work... it just feels impossible sometimes. So, no, not depressed. Just... really, really irritated that I can't seem to make my brain cooperate when it comes to the boring, sit-down stuff. It's like trying to drive a car with the parking brake on, you know? You're pressing the gas, but you're just not going anywhere, and you can smell the brakes burning, and it's just a giant hassle. But it doesn't make me sad. Just annoyed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find your mind wandering when you're in the middle of a task?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. All the time. It's actually a huge problem for me, especially in meetings. And I have to sit through a lot of meetings in my role, as you can probably imagine. Like, just last Tuesday, we had this big cross-clinic leadership meeting. We were going over the quarterly budget updates, which, again, is important stuff that I really need to know for my own reporting. \n\nSo I'm sitting there, and I'm really trying to listen to the finance director—he's this guy named Tom, nice guy, but he has this very monotone voice, which doesn't help. But anyway, I'm trying to focus on what he's saying about the Q3 projections. And then, someone's phone vibrates on the table. It wasn't even a ring, just that little *bzz bzz*, and instantly, my brain is just... gone. I'm looking at the phone, wondering whose it is, wondering if it's an emergency at one of the clinics. And then while I'm thinking about that, I hear some nurses talking out in the hallway. I can't quite make out what they're saying, but I can hear the cadence of it, and I start trying to figure out who's talking and what they're discussing. \n\nOr, even worse, it's not even an outside noise. It's my own brain. Like, in that same meeting, after the phone thing, I suddenly remembered that I forgot to email the guys in my weekend softball league about the schedule change for Sunday. And once that thought entered my head, it was like a train leaving the station. I'm sitting there, physically in the room, looking right at Tom, but mentally I'm drafting the email to the team, thinking about who can play first base since Dave is out of town, and wondering if we have enough softballs in the equipment bag. \n\nAnd then, suddenly, I snap back to reality, and I realize Tom has moved on to the next slide, and I have absolutely no idea what he just said for the last three or four minutes. I completely missed the part about the new supply chain vendor, which I really needed to know. It's so frustrating because I'm not trying to ignore him, and I'm not trying to be disrespectful. My mind just... gets hijacked by whatever little thing pops up, whether it's a sound in the room or just a random thought in my head. And it takes me a minute to even realize that I've drifted off. So, yeah, wandering mind? Definitely. It's a constant battle.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Act on impulse? No, not really. I mean, I'm not one of those guys who just up and buys a sports car on a Tuesday or, you know, decides to go skydiving on a whim. I'm actually pretty predictable in that regard. My wife always jokes that I'm a creature of habit. I eat the same turkey sandwich for lunch almost every day—Boar's Head turkey, provolone, a little bit of mayo on wheat bread, usually from that deli down the street from the main clinic. \n\nBut as far as making impulsive decisions at work or in life, no, I don't really do that. I mean, in my job, you can't really afford to be impulsive, right? As a manager, especially in healthcare, everything has a protocol. If a patient complains or if there's a staffing shortage because three nurses called out sick with the flu—which actually happened last winter, it was a nightmare, we had to pull staff from two other locations just to keep the urgent care side open—you have to follow the procedures. You can't just fly off the handle or make snap decisions without thinking about the downstream effects. \n\nAnd even financially, like I said, I'm dealing with budgets and variance reports. I don't just randomly approve a purchase order for a new EKG machine without running it up the flagpole first. I'm actually pretty careful about that stuff. If anything, I might overthink things sometimes, or just put them off, like we talked about earlier. But I don't just blurt things out in meetings or make wild choices without thinking. I'm pretty grounded, honestly. It's just the focusing and the starting that trip me up, not doing things too fast or without thinking. So, yeah, no real issues with impulsivity.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Restless or fidgety? Hmm. You know, not really. I mean, I'm not bouncing off the walls or anything. I don't sit there shaking my leg up and down like a jackhammer, which I know some people do. My brother-in-law, for instance, he cannot sit still at a family dinner to save his life. He's always tapping the table or jiggling his knee, and it honestly drives my sister crazy. But no, I don't really do that.\n\nI mean, sure, if I'm stuck in one of those three-hour strategic planning sessions—and we have those every quarter, usually in this conference room that has the most uncomfortable chairs known to man, they're those stiff mesh ones that look ergonomic but really just dig into your lower back—I'll shift around in my seat. I'll cross my legs, uncross them, maybe stretch my back a little bit. But I think anyone would do that, right? You sit in a bad chair for three hours listening to someone talk about synergy and patient retention metrics, you're going to want to move around a little. \n\nBut I don't feel this, like, internal need to move. I'm not crawling out of my skin if I have to sit still. When I'm at my desk trying to force myself to look at those variance reports, I'm sitting perfectly still. I'm just staring at the monitor, feeling annoyed with myself, like I said before. But my body isn't restless. I'm not pacing the floor of my office or spinning in my chair or anything like that. \n\nEven when I'm at home, you know, watching TV in the evening. We usually watch a couple of episodes of whatever show we're currently binging—right now it's that new crime drama on streaming, the one set in Chicago, it's pretty good, although the medical scenes are always so inaccurate it makes me laugh. But anyway, I can just sit on the couch for two hours straight, no problem. I might get up to grab a glass of water or let the dog out—we have a golden retriever, Buster, he's great but he always wants to go out right in the middle of an episode—but I'm not fidgeting the whole time. So, yeah, I'd say physically, I'm pretty calm. It's just my brain that won't sit still, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to sustain your attention on tasks that require continuous mental effort?\n\nPatient: Oh, man. Yes. Honestly... that right there is the main reason I’m here talking to you today. It’s exactly that. Anything that takes real, continuous, sitting-down brain work—it just feels like I hit a massive brick wall. \n\nIf I’m walking around the clinic, putting out fires, dealing with a scheduling mix-up or a patient complaint, I’m totally fine. But the heavy mental lifting? It’s a completely different story. Like those monthly reports I mentioned earlier. I have to pull data from three different scheduling systems because we still haven’t unified our software—which, by the way, is a whole other headache we’ve been arguing with corporate about for two years. They keep promising this new system rollout, but it just keeps getting delayed, so we're stuck doing it the hard way. Anyway, I have to sit there, compile all this raw data, and write a detailed narrative explaining, you know, why overtime was up fifteen percent in urgent care last month. \n\nOr another huge one is planning our multi-site health events. We do this big community outreach thing every fall—free flu shots, blood pressure screenings, diabetes education—and it requires bringing in staff from four different clinics to run the tents. It’s incredibly important stuff. I know it matters, the community relies on it, and my bosses expect it to be flawless. But when I sit down at my desk to actually do the logistical planning for it, to just focus and grind out the permit applications or the volunteer schedules... my brain just refuses to engage. \n\nI will do literally anything else to avoid that sustained mental effort. I’ll reorganize my email folders, I’ll go out to the nurses' station and restock the printer paper, I’ll end up chatting with the front desk receptionist for twenty minutes about her kid’s travel soccer team. I just keep putting the big tasks off and putting them off, even when I know how critical they are. It’s not that I don’t know how to do the work—I’ve been managing these clinics for years—but the actual act of sitting there and forcing my brain to stay on track for that long... it's like trying to push a boulder up a hill. I just avoid it until the panic of the deadline finally kicks in, and it's just exhausting living like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your time and prioritizing tasks?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. That is... yeah, that's a huge struggle for me. Organizing and prioritizing is probably where things fall apart the most. \n\nIt’s like, when I’m juggling all my regular work projects—the staffing, the variance reports, all that day-to-day clinic stuff—and then on top of that, I’m helping out with this memorial committee stuff for my uncle's old VFW post, plus those big community outreach events I just mentioned... it’s just too many moving parts. And my system for keeping track of it all is, well, it's not really a system at all. I’ll literally have notes in three different places. I’ll write something down on a yellow legal pad during a morning meeting, and then later I’ll jot a reminder on a Post-it note and stick it to my steering wheel so I don't forget it on the drive home, and then I’ll also have a random, half-finished list going in the Notes app on my iPhone. \n\nMy wife actually bought me one of those expensive leather-bound planners for Christmas two years ago—really nice quality, monogrammed and everything—thinking it would help me consolidate all these floating pieces of paper. I think I used it for about three days before I buried it under a pile of mail on the kitchen counter, and now I don't even know where it is. \n\nBut anyway, because everything is scattered across all these different places, when I actually sit down and try to plan my day, I just draw a blank. I look at all of it and I can’t seem to figure out what needs to happen first. Is it more important to call the caterer for the memorial dinner, or do I need to finalize the permit for the flu clinic first? They both feel equally urgent and completely overwhelming at the same time, so my brain just sort of short-circuits. I can't figure out the logical order of operations. \n\nSo what happens is, I just kind of spin my wheels. I'll bounce back and forth, maybe do a tiny piece of one thing, then get distracted by an email, and I never actually set a real priority. And because I can't line them up in order of importance, I end up rushing frantically at the very end to get it all done. It’s incredibly stressful. I mean, just last week I was literally sitting in my car outside the grocery store, frantically typing out emails on my phone for the memorial committee because the printer needed the program draft by 5 PM, and I had completely lost track of time while I was trying to sort out a vendor contract for work. I always manage to pull it off somehow, but the rushing at the end is just exhausting. It feels like I'm always one step away from dropping a major ball because I can't just organize a simple timeline.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions when faced with frustrating situations?\n\nPatient: Managing my emotions? Hmm. No, I wouldn't say that. I mean... I definitely get frustrated, don't get me wrong. Anyone working in healthcare administration right now is frustrated on a daily basis. It’s just the nature of the beast. \n\nLike, just the other day, we had this whole fiasco with a vendor delivering the wrong gauge needles for our phlebotomy lab. We ordered 21-gauge, they sent 25-gauge, which, you know, is practically useless for standard blood draws on adults, it just takes forever and you risk hemolyzing the sample. And when I called the rep—this guy named Gary who is always, always messing up our orders, I swear I don't know how he still has his territory—he tried to tell me over the phone that I was the one who keyed it in wrong. Which I absolutely didn't, I had the carbon copy of the purchase order right in front of me. \n\nSo, was I frustrated? Oh, incredibly. My blood pressure was probably through the roof, and I had three phlebotomists standing outside my office door waiting to know what to do because they had a waiting room full of fasting patients who were getting crankier by the minute. But I didn't lose my temper. I didn't yell at Gary, even though I really, really wanted to ask him how he manages to tie his shoes in the morning. I didn't slam the phone down or snap at my staff. I just took a deep breath, calmly explained that I was looking at the PO with the correct item number, and told him he needed to courier the right supplies over from their regional warehouse within the hour. \n\nI actually pride myself on that, honestly. Being the calm one in the storm. You kind of have to be when you're managing a clinic. If the doctors are stressed and the nurses are stressed, the manager can't be flying off the handle or breaking down. So no, I don't really struggle with my emotions. I keep things pretty steady. I might complain to my wife about Gary when I get home, maybe vent for twenty minutes while I'm chopping vegetables for dinner or taking the dog for a walk, but in the moment? No, I keep a pretty level head. I don't really have those big emotional outbursts or get visibly angry. It's really just the focus and the organizing that I can't seem to get a grip on.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Anything that takes real, continuous, sitting-down brain work—it just feels like I hit a massive brick wall. \n\nIf I’m walking around the clinic, putting out fires, dealing with a scheduling mix-up or a patient complaint, I’m totally fine. I have to pull data from three different scheduling systems because we still haven’t unified our software—which, by the way, is a whole other headache we’ve been arguing with corporate about for two years. They keep promising this new system rollout, but it just keeps getting delayed, so we're stuck doing it the hard way. Anyway, I have to sit there, compile all this raw data, and write a detailed narrative explaining, you know, why overtime was up fifteen percent in urgent care last month. \n\nOr another huge one is planning our multi-site health events. We do this big community outreach thing every fall—free flu shots, blood pressure screenings, diabetes education—and it requires bringing in staff from four different clinics to run the tents. It’s incredibly important stuff. I know it matters, the community relies on it, and my bosses expect it to be flawless. I’ll reorganize my email folders, I’ll go out to the nurses' station and restock the printer paper, I’ll end up chatting with the front desk receptionist for twenty minutes about her kid’s travel soccer team. I just keep putting the big tasks off and putting them off, even when I know how critical they are. It’s not that I don’t know how to do the work—I’ve been managing these clinics for years—but the actual act of sitting there and forcing my brain to stay on track for that long... it's like trying to push a boulder up a hill. I just avoid it until the panic of the deadline finally kicks in, and it's just exhausting living like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your time and prioritizing tasks?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. And my system for keeping track of it all is, well, it's not really a system at all. I’ll literally have notes in three different places. I’ll write something down on a yellow legal pad during a morning meeting, and then later I’ll jot a reminder on a Post-it note and stick it to my steering wheel so I don't forget it on the drive home, and then I’ll also have a random, half-finished list going in the Notes app on my iPhone. \n\nMy wife actually bought me one of those expensive leather-bound planners for Christmas two years ago—really nice quality, monogrammed and everything—thinking it would help me consolidate all these floating pieces of paper. I think I used it for about three days before I buried it under a pile of mail on the kitchen counter, and now I don't even know where it is. \n\nBut anyway, because everything is scattered across all these different places, when I actually sit down and try to plan my day, I just draw a blank. I can't figure out the logical order of operations. \n\nSo what happens is, I just kind of spin my wheels. I mean, just last week I was literally sitting in my car outside the grocery store, frantically typing out emails on my phone for the memorial committee because the printer needed the program draft by 5 PM, and I had completely lost track of time while I was trying to sort out a vendor contract for work. It feels like I'm always one step away from dropping a major ball because I can't just organize a simple timeline.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions when faced with frustrating situations?\n\nPatient: Managing my emotions? Hmm. No, I wouldn't say that. I mean... I definitely get frustrated, don't get me wrong. Anyone working in healthcare administration right now is frustrated on a daily basis. It’s just the nature of the beast. \n\nLike, just the other day, we had this whole fiasco with a vendor delivering the wrong gauge needles for our phlebotomy lab. We ordered 21-gauge, they sent 25-gauge, which, you know, is practically useless for standard blood draws on adults, it just takes forever and you risk hemolyzing the sample. And when I called the rep—this guy named Gary who is always, always messing up our orders, I swear I don't know how he still has his territory—he tried to tell me over the phone that I was the one who keyed it in wrong. Which I absolutely didn't, I had the carbon copy of the purchase order right in front of me. \n\nSo, was I frustrated? Oh, incredibly. My blood pressure was probably through the roof, and I had three phlebotomists standing outside my office door waiting to know what to do because they had a waiting room full of fasting patients who were getting crankier by the minute. I just took a deep breath, calmly explained that I was looking at the PO with the correct item number, and told him he needed to courier the right supplies over from their regional warehouse within the hour. \n\nI actually pride myself on that, honestly. Being the calm one in the storm. You kind of have to be when you're managing a clinic. If the doctors are stressed and the nurses are stressed, the manager can't be flying off the handle or breaking down. So no, I don't really struggle with my emotions. I keep things pretty steady. I might complain to my wife about Gary when I get home, maybe vent for twenty minutes while I'm chopping vegetables for dinner or taking the dog for a walk, but in the moment?     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Leaving them unfinished? No... no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I definitely put things off, like I was saying before with the event permits and the monthly reports, but I don't just abandon them halfway through. 

In my line of work, you really can't do that. You can't just leave a staff credentialing packet half-finished because you lost interest, you know? Like, if one of our physician assistants—let's say her state license renewal isn't processed because I got distracted and just walked away from the portal—she literally cannot see patients the next day. The whole schedule falls apart, revenue drops, the medical director gets involved, and patients are furious. The stakes are just way too high in healthcare administration. So I always finish what I need to do. Always. 

It’s just... the way I get there is a complete nightmare. I might delay and delay, and I might get distracted by ten different minor things—like fixing the jammed copier or chatting with the security guard—*before* I sit down to do the actual work. But once that deadline panic finally sets in, I am locked in until it's done. I might be sitting at my kitchen island at 11:30 at night, surrounded by sticky notes, drinking my third cup of coffee and stressing out my wife because I'm furiously typing up a Joint Commission readiness report that's due at 8 AM... but it gets done. 

Honestly, my bosses and the committee members probably consider me a very reliable guy because I always deliver the final product. They just don't see the absolute chaos behind the scenes. They don't see the late nights or the frantic rushing. Now, if we're talking about hobbies or stuff around the house? Sure, maybe. I bought a whole homebrewing kit a few years ago, spent a fortune on the carboys and the hops, used it exactly one time, and now it's just collecting dust in the corner of the garage. But with actual work or committee responsibilities? No, I never leave them hanging. I push through and finish them, even if the process is completely exhausting.

Oh, man. Yes. That... honestly, that right there is the main reason I’m sitting in this chair talking to you today. It’s exactly that. When it comes to anything that takes real, sustained, sitting-down brain work, it’s like I just physically hit a wall.

But when I actually sit down at my desk, close my door, open up my laptop, and stare at that blank document or that massive Excel grid... I just can't do it. My brain just sort of rebels. It's like this invisible, impenetrable barrier drops down in front of me. So instead of just starting, I’ll find literally anything else to do. I’ll go walk the halls, I'll go chat with the front desk staff, I'll even go restock the medium-sized nitrile gloves in exam room three—which is definitely not a clinic manager's job, but I’ll do it just to avoid the heavy lifting of that mental work. I just keep putting it off and putting it off, day after day, even though I am fully aware of the deadlines looming over my head. It’s exhausting, honestly, knowing you have to do something so important and just... not being able to make yourself start.

And when I called the rep—this guy named Gary who is always, always messing up our orders, I swear I don't know how he still has his territory—he tried to tell me over the phone that I was the one who keyed it in wrong. Which I absolutely didn't, I had the carbon copy of the purchase order right in front of me. \n\nSo, was I frustrated? Oh, incredibly. My blood pressure was probably through the roof, and I had three phlebotomists standing outside my office door waiting to know what to do because they had a waiting room full of fasting patients who were getting crankier by the minute. But I didn't lose my temper. I didn't yell at Gary, even though I really, really wanted to ask him how he manages to tie his shoes in the morning. I didn't slam the phone down or snap at my staff. I just took a deep breath, calmly explained that I was looking at the PO with the correct item number, and told him he needed to courier the right supplies over from their regional warehouse within the hour. \n\nI actually pride myself on that, honestly. Being the calm one in the storm. You kind of have to be when you're managing a clinic. If the doctors are stressed and the nurses are stressed, the manager can't be flying off the handle or breaking down. So no, I don't really struggle with my emotions. I keep things pretty steady. I might complain to my wife about Gary when I get home, maybe vent for twenty minutes while I'm chopping vegetables for dinner or taking the dog for a walk, but in the moment? No, I keep a pretty level head. I don't really have those big emotional outbursts or get visibly angry. It's really just the focus and the organizing that I can't seem to get a grip on.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself leaving tasks unfinished because you get distracted or lose interest?\n\nPatient: Leaving them unfinished? No... no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I definitely put things off, like I was saying before with the event permits and the monthly reports, but I don't just abandon them halfway through. \n\nIn my line of work, you really can't do that. You can't just leave a staff credentialing packet half-finished because you lost interest, you know? Like, if one of our physician assistants—let's say her state license renewal isn't processed because I got distracted and just walked away from the portal—she literally cannot see patients the next day. The whole schedule falls apart, revenue drops, the medical director gets involved, and patients are furious. The stakes are just way too high in healthcare administration. So I always finish what I need to do. Always. \n\nIt’s just... the way I get there is a complete nightmare. I might delay and delay, and I might get distracted by ten different minor things—like fixing the jammed copier or chatting with the security guard—*before* I sit down to do the actual work. But once that deadline panic finally sets in, I am locked in until it's done. I might be sitting at my kitchen island at 11:30 at night, surrounded by sticky notes, drinking my third cup of coffee and stressing out my wife because I'm furiously typing up a Joint Commission readiness report that's due at 8 AM... but it gets done. \n\nHonestly, my bosses and the committee members probably consider me a very reliable guy because I always deliver the final product. They just don't see the absolute chaos behind the scenes. They don't see the late nights or the frantic rushing. Now, if we're talking about hobbies or stuff around the house? Sure, maybe. I bought a whole homebrewing kit a few years ago, spent a fortune on the carboys and the hops, used it exactly one time, and now it's just collecting dust in the corner of the garage. But with actual work or committee responsibilities? No, I never leave them hanging. I push through and finish them, even if the process is completely exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to motivate yourself to start tasks when you're not interested in them?\n\nPatient: Oh, man. Yes. That... honestly, that right there is the main reason I’m sitting in this chair talking to you today. It’s exactly that. When it comes to anything that takes real, sustained, sitting-down brain work, it’s like I just physically hit a wall. \n\nTake the monthly reports, for example. Every single month, I have to do these complex variance reports and budget reconciliations for the clinic. I have to pull data from our electronic health records, cross-reference it with the billing department’s spreadsheets, and write up a whole summary for the board. Or, you know, planning this massive multi-site health event we do every fall—which, by the way, is a logistical nightmare because we’re coordinating mobile vaccination units across three different counties, trying to get city permits, scheduling volunteer nurses... it’s a huge undertaking. And I know it matters. I know exactly how crucial it is for the community and for our annual funding. \n\nBut when I actually sit down at my desk, close my door, open up my laptop, and stare at that blank document or that massive Excel grid... I just can't do it. My brain just sort of rebels. It's like this invisible, impenetrable barrier drops down in front of me. So instead of just starting, I’ll find literally anything else to do. I’ll go walk the halls, I'll go chat with the front desk staff, I'll even go restock the medium-sized nitrile gloves in exam room three—which is definitely not a clinic manager's job, but I’ll do it just to avoid the heavy lifting of that mental work. I just keep putting it off and putting it off, day after day, even though I am fully aware of the deadlines looming over my head. It’s exhausting, honestly, knowing you have to do something so important and just... not being able to make yourself start.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I feel like... well, honestly, that’s pretty much the same as the frustration thing we talked about earlier. Stress and frustration, they kind of go hand-in-hand in my line of work, and my answer is pretty much the same. No, I don't have problems controlling my emotions when I'm stressed. 

If anything, stress just pushes me into this sort of... crisis management mode. I mean, my baseline at the clinic is basically a constant state of moderate stress anyway, so you get used to it. Like, take last Tuesday, for example. We had an absolute perfect storm. Two of our medical assistants called out sick with the flu—or maybe it was RSV, whatever it is, it’s going around the schools right now and knocking everyone out—and then right at 9:00 AM, right when our waiting room is at maximum capacity with the morning rush, our entire electronic medical record system goes completely offline. Just... boom. Blank screens across the entire clinic. Server error. 

Now, the physicians, they immediately start panicking because they can't see the patient histories or put in lab orders, and the front desk staff is getting yelled at by patients who have been waiting forty-five minutes past their appointment times. The stress level in that building was palpable. You could literally cut it with a knife. And internally? Sure, my stomach is in absolute knots. I'm thinking about the revenue we're losing, the angry patient satisfaction surveys we're going to get, the absolute nightmare it's going to be for my staff to back-enter all those paper charts once the system finally comes back up. 

But did I lose control of my emotions? Did I yell or freak out or shut down? Not at all. I just immediately pivoted. I grabbed the emergency downtime binders—which, thank goodness, I had actually updated myself just two months prior—and I started physically handing out paper forms to the providers. I was walking up and down the halls, checking in with the nurses, keeping my voice completely level, reassuring the patients in the waiting room. I spent two solid hours on hold with corporate IT, just sitting there listening to that awful, tinny elevator hold music, and I didn't snap at the poor tier-one tech when they finally answered the phone, even though he was asking me completely unhelpful questions about whether my computer was plugged in. 

So, no. I don't lose my cool under stress. I just bottle it up, deal with the crisis step by step, and then maybe, you know, have a stiff drink and complain to my wife when I finally get home at seven o'clock. But in the moment, when the pressure is on? I'm a rock. I have to be. If the manager loses it, the whole clinic falls apart. So the emotional side of things, the stress, the frustration... that's really not the issue for me. It's just the day-to-day focus stuff that's killing me.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.0, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thanks for seeing me. Yeah, so, what brings me in... well, it’s been building up for a while now. I’m a medical services manager, so I oversee operations for a few different clinics, which means there’s always a lot of moving parts. I'm usually fine when I'm on my feet, you know, putting out fires and talking to the nursing staff or dealing with patient complaints. \n\nBut the main reason I’m here is that anything that takes real, sitting-down brain work... I just feel like I hit a wall. Like, a literal brick wall. For instance, I have to do these detailed monthly reports on our budget variances, or right now I'm supposed to be planning this big multi-site community health event, and I just keep putting it off. I keep pushing it to the bottom of the pile, even when I know it really, really matters. \n\nI know the deadlines are looming, I know my directors need those numbers, but the thought of just sitting at my desk and focusing on that kind of heavy mental task... my brain just completely rejects it. It’s incredibly frustrating because I’ve worked really hard to get to this level in my career, especially having started out with just my associate's degree and having to prove myself over the years, and now I just can't seem to force myself to do the most important parts of my job. I'll find a hundred other little things to do instead of just sitting down and doing the one big thing I actually need to do. It's just getting to be too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about doing those detailed reports or planning the health event?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, I wouldn't say it's anxiety. I mean, I know what anxiety feels like, my sister has terrible anxiety, and she gets the racing heart and the sweating and all that, and it's not like that for me at all. It's more just... a complete lack of ability to start. \n\nLike, when I think about the budget variance report, which is due on the fifth of every month, by the way, and requires me to pull data from three different scheduling systems and cross-reference it with our billing software... it's not that I'm scared of the report. I know how to do it. I've done it a dozen times. But when I look at the icon on my desktop, or when I open the Excel file, it's just this overwhelming feeling of... ugh. Just, ugh. My brain just slides right off it. \n\nI don't sit there worrying about whether I'll do a bad job or if the directors will be mad at me—well, I mean, I do worry about them being mad if it's late, but the actual task itself doesn't make me nervous. It's just so incredibly tedious and requires so much sustained attention, and I just find myself thinking, \"Oh, I should really check if the maintenance guy fixed that leaky faucet in clinic B,\" or \"I need to reply to that email from the vendor about the new gloves.\" And before I know it, two hours have passed, and I haven't typed a single number into the spreadsheet. So, no, not anxious. Just... completely unable to make myself do it, if that makes sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed when you're unable to start or complete these tasks?\n\nPatient: Sad or depressed? No, I really wouldn't use those words. I mean, sure, I get frustrated, like I was saying before, because I know I should just be able to do it, you know? But it's not a sadness. It's not like I'm sitting at my desk crying over the budget report or feeling hopeless about my life. I actually really like my life, and I like my job—most of it, anyway. \n\nI'm not walking around feeling down in the dumps or, I don't know, empty or anything like that. It's more of an annoyance with myself. Like, I'll be sitting there, staring at the screen, and I'll think, \"Come on, man, just do the thing.\" But my brain is just stubbornly refusing. And then I get mad at myself for wasting time, especially when I know I'm perfectly capable of doing the work. I mean, I didn't get to be a manager by being stupid, right? I started out at the front desk with just my associate's degree and worked my way up, so I know how to work hard. \n\nBut this specific kind of work... it just feels impossible sometimes. So, no, not depressed. Just... really, really irritated that I can't seem to make my brain cooperate when it comes to the boring, sit-down stuff. It's like trying to drive a car with the parking brake on, you know? You're pressing the gas, but you're just not going anywhere, and you can smell the brakes burning, and it's just a giant hassle. But it doesn't make me sad. Just annoyed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find your mind wandering when you're in the middle of a task?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. All the time. It's actually a huge problem for me, especially in meetings. And I have to sit through a lot of meetings in my role, as you can probably imagine. Like, just last Tuesday, we had this big cross-clinic leadership meeting. We were going over the quarterly budget updates, which, again, is important stuff that I really need to know for my own reporting. \n\nSo I'm sitting there, and I'm really trying to listen to the finance director—he's this guy named Tom, nice guy, but he has this very monotone voice, which doesn't help. But anyway, I'm trying to focus on what he's saying about the Q3 projections. And then, someone's phone vibrates on the table. It wasn't even a ring, just that little *bzz bzz*, and instantly, my brain is just... gone. I'm looking at the phone, wondering whose it is, wondering if it's an emergency at one of the clinics. And then while I'm thinking about that, I hear some nurses talking out in the hallway. I can't quite make out what they're saying, but I can hear the cadence of it, and I start trying to figure out who's talking and what they're discussing. \n\nOr, even worse, it's not even an outside noise. It's my own brain. Like, in that same meeting, after the phone thing, I suddenly remembered that I forgot to email the guys in my weekend softball league about the schedule change for Sunday. And once that thought entered my head, it was like a train leaving the station. I'm sitting there, physically in the room, looking right at Tom, but mentally I'm drafting the email to the team, thinking about who can play first base since Dave is out of town, and wondering if we have enough softballs in the equipment bag. \n\nAnd then, suddenly, I snap back to reality, and I realize Tom has moved on to the next slide, and I have absolutely no idea what he just said for the last three or four minutes. I completely missed the part about the new supply chain vendor, which I really needed to know. It's so frustrating because I'm not trying to ignore him, and I'm not trying to be disrespectful. My mind just... gets hijacked by whatever little thing pops up, whether it's a sound in the room or just a random thought in my head. And it takes me a minute to even realize that I've drifted off. So, yeah, wandering mind? Definitely. It's a constant battle.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Act on impulse? No, not really. I mean, I'm not one of those guys who just up and buys a sports car on a Tuesday or, you know, decides to go skydiving on a whim. I'm actually pretty predictable in that regard. My wife always jokes that I'm a creature of habit. I eat the same turkey sandwich for lunch almost every day—Boar's Head turkey, provolone, a little bit of mayo on wheat bread, usually from that deli down the street from the main clinic. \n\nBut as far as making impulsive decisions at work or in life, no, I don't really do that. I mean, in my job, you can't really afford to be impulsive, right? As a manager, especially in healthcare, everything has a protocol. If a patient complains or if there's a staffing shortage because three nurses called out sick with the flu—which actually happened last winter, it was a nightmare, we had to pull staff from two other locations just to keep the urgent care side open—you have to follow the procedures. You can't just fly off the handle or make snap decisions without thinking about the downstream effects. \n\nAnd even financially, like I said, I'm dealing with budgets and variance reports. I don't just randomly approve a purchase order for a new EKG machine without running it up the flagpole first. I'm actually pretty careful about that stuff. If anything, I might overthink things sometimes, or just put them off, like we talked about earlier. But I don't just blurt things out in meetings or make wild choices without thinking. I'm pretty grounded, honestly. It's just the focusing and the starting that trip me up, not doing things too fast or without thinking. So, yeah, no real issues with impulsivity.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Restless or fidgety? Hmm. You know, not really. I mean, I'm not bouncing off the walls or anything. I don't sit there shaking my leg up and down like a jackhammer, which I know some people do. My brother-in-law, for instance, he cannot sit still at a family dinner to save his life. He's always tapping the table or jiggling his knee, and it honestly drives my sister crazy. But no, I don't really do that.\n\nI mean, sure, if I'm stuck in one of those three-hour strategic planning sessions—and we have those every quarter, usually in this conference room that has the most uncomfortable chairs known to man, they're those stiff mesh ones that look ergonomic but really just dig into your lower back—I'll shift around in my seat. I'll cross my legs, uncross them, maybe stretch my back a little bit. But I think anyone would do that, right? You sit in a bad chair for three hours listening to someone talk about synergy and patient retention metrics, you're going to want to move around a little. \n\nBut I don't feel this, like, internal need to move. I'm not crawling out of my skin if I have to sit still. When I'm at my desk trying to force myself to look at those variance reports, I'm sitting perfectly still. I'm just staring at the monitor, feeling annoyed with myself, like I said before. But my body isn't restless. I'm not pacing the floor of my office or spinning in my chair or anything like that. \n\nEven when I'm at home, you know, watching TV in the evening. We usually watch a couple of episodes of whatever show we're currently binging—right now it's that new crime drama on streaming, the one set in Chicago, it's pretty good, although the medical scenes are always so inaccurate it makes me laugh. But anyway, I can just sit on the couch for two hours straight, no problem. I might get up to grab a glass of water or let the dog out—we have a golden retriever, Buster, he's great but he always wants to go out right in the middle of an episode—but I'm not fidgeting the whole time. So, yeah, I'd say physically, I'm pretty calm. It's just my brain that won't sit still, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to sustain your attention on tasks that require continuous mental effort?\n\nPatient: Oh, man. Yes. Honestly... that right there is the main reason I’m here talking to you today. It’s exactly that. Anything that takes real, continuous, sitting-down brain work—it just feels like I hit a massive brick wall. \n\nIf I’m walking around the clinic, putting out fires, dealing with a scheduling mix-up or a patient complaint, I’m totally fine. But the heavy mental lifting? It’s a completely different story. Like those monthly reports I mentioned earlier. I have to pull data from three different scheduling systems because we still haven’t unified our software—which, by the way, is a whole other headache we’ve been arguing with corporate about for two years. They keep promising this new system rollout, but it just keeps getting delayed, so we're stuck doing it the hard way. Anyway, I have to sit there, compile all this raw data, and write a detailed narrative explaining, you know, why overtime was up fifteen percent in urgent care last month. \n\nOr another huge one is planning our multi-site health events. We do this big community outreach thing every fall—free flu shots, blood pressure screenings, diabetes education—and it requires bringing in staff from four different clinics to run the tents. It’s incredibly important stuff. I know it matters, the community relies on it, and my bosses expect it to be flawless. But when I sit down at my desk to actually do the logistical planning for it, to just focus and grind out the permit applications or the volunteer schedules... my brain just refuses to engage. \n\nI will do literally anything else to avoid that sustained mental effort. I’ll reorganize my email folders, I’ll go out to the nurses' station and restock the printer paper, I’ll end up chatting with the front desk receptionist for twenty minutes about her kid’s travel soccer team. I just keep putting the big tasks off and putting them off, even when I know how critical they are. It’s not that I don’t know how to do the work—I’ve been managing these clinics for years—but the actual act of sitting there and forcing my brain to stay on track for that long... it's like trying to push a boulder up a hill. I just avoid it until the panic of the deadline finally kicks in, and it's just exhausting living like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your time and prioritizing tasks?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. That is... yeah, that's a huge struggle for me. Organizing and prioritizing is probably where things fall apart the most. \n\nIt’s like, when I’m juggling all my regular work projects—the staffing, the variance reports, all that day-to-day clinic stuff—and then on top of that, I’m helping out with this memorial committee stuff for my uncle's old VFW post, plus those big community outreach events I just mentioned... it’s just too many moving parts. I think I used it for about three days before I buried it under a pile of mail on the kitchen counter, and now I don't even know where it is. \n\nBut anyway, because everything is scattered across all these different places, when I actually sit down and try to plan my day, I just draw a blank. Is it more important to call the caterer for the memorial dinner, or do I need to finalize the permit for the flu clinic first? They both feel equally urgent and completely overwhelming at the same time, so my brain just sort of short-circuits. I can't figure out the logical order of operations. \n\nSo what happens is, I just kind of spin my wheels. I'll bounce back and forth, maybe do a tiny piece of one thing, then get distracted by an email, and I never actually set a real priority. I always manage to pull it off somehow, but the rushing at the end is just exhausting. I just took a deep breath, calmly explained that I was looking at the PO with the correct item number, and told him he needed to courier the right supplies over from their regional warehouse within the hour. \n\nI actually pride myself on that, honestly. Being the calm one in the storm. You kind of have to be when you're managing a clinic. If the doctors are stressed and the nurses are stressed, the manager can't be flying off the handle or breaking down. So no, I don't really struggle with my emotions. I keep things pretty steady. I might complain to my wife about Gary when I get home, maybe vent for twenty minutes while I'm chopping vegetables for dinner or taking the dog for a walk, but in the moment? I think I used it for about three days before I buried it under a pile of mail on the kitchen counter, and now I don't even know where it is. \n\nBut anyway, because everything is scattered across all these different places, when I actually sit down and try to plan my day, I just draw a blank. I look at all of it and I can’t seem to figure out what needs to happen first. Is it more important to call the caterer for the memorial dinner, or do I need to finalize the permit for the flu clinic first? They both feel equally urgent and completely overwhelming at the same time, so my brain just sort of short-circuits. I can't figure out the logical order of operations. \n\nSo what happens is, I just kind of spin my wheels. I'll bounce back and forth, maybe do a tiny piece of one thing, then get distracted by an email, and I never actually set a real priority. And because I can't line them up in order of importance, I end up rushing frantically at the very end to get it all done. It’s incredibly stressful. I mean, just last week I was literally sitting in my car outside the grocery store, frantically typing out emails on my phone for the memorial committee because the printer needed the program draft by 5 PM, and I had completely lost track of time while I was trying to sort out a vendor contract for work. I always manage to pull it off somehow, but the rushing at the end is just exhausting. It feels like I'm always one step away from dropping a major ball because I can't just organize a simple timeline.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions when faced with frustrating situations?\n\nPatient: Managing my emotions? Hmm. No, I wouldn't say that. I mean... I definitely get frustrated, don't get me wrong. Anyone working in healthcare administration right now is frustrated on a daily basis. It’s just the nature of the beast. \n\nLike, just the other day, we had this whole fiasco with a vendor delivering the wrong gauge needles for our phlebotomy lab. We ordered 21-gauge, they sent 25-gauge, which, you know, is practically useless for standard blood draws on adults, it just takes forever and you risk hemolyzing the sample. And when I called the rep—this guy named Gary who is always, always messing up our orders, I swear I don't know how he still has his territory—he tried to tell me over the phone that I was the one who keyed it in wrong. Which I absolutely didn't, I had the carbon copy of the purchase order right in front of me. \n\nSo, was I frustrated? Oh, incredibly. My blood pressure was probably through the roof, and I had three phlebotomists standing outside my office door waiting to know what to do because they had a waiting room full of fasting patients who were getting crankier by the minute. But I didn't lose my temper. I didn't yell at Gary, even though I really, really wanted to ask him how he manages to tie his shoes in the morning. I didn't slam the phone down or snap at my staff. I just took a deep breath, calmly explained that I was looking at the PO with the correct item number, and told him he needed to courier the right supplies over from their regional warehouse within the hour. \n\nI actually pride myself on that, honestly. Being the calm one in the storm. You kind of have to be when you're managing a clinic. If the doctors are stressed and the nurses are stressed, the manager can't be flying off the handle or breaking down. So no, I don't really struggle with my emotions. I keep things pretty steady. I might complain to my wife about Gary when I get home, maybe vent for twenty minutes while I'm chopping vegetables for dinner or taking the dog for a walk, but in the moment? No, I keep a pretty level head. I don't really have those big emotional outbursts or get visibly angry. It's really just the focus and the organizing that I can't seem to get a grip on.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself leaving tasks unfinished because you get distracted or lose interest?\n\nPatient: Leaving them unfinished? No... no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, I definitely put things off, like I was saying before with the event permits and the monthly reports, but I don't just abandon them halfway through. \n\nIn my line of work, you really can't do that. You can't just leave a staff credentialing packet half-finished because you lost interest, you know? Like, if one of our physician assistants—let's say her state license renewal isn't processed because I got distracted and just walked away from the portal—she literally cannot see patients the next day. The whole schedule falls apart, revenue drops, the medical director gets involved, and patients are furious. The stakes are just way too high in healthcare administration. So I always finish what I need to do. Always. \n\nIt’s just... the way I get there is a complete nightmare. I might delay and delay, and I might get distracted by ten different minor things—like fixing the jammed copier or chatting with the security guard—*before* I sit down to do the actual work. But once that deadline panic finally sets in, I am locked in until it's done. I might be sitting at my kitchen island at 11:30 at night, surrounded by sticky notes, drinking my third cup of coffee and stressing out my wife because I'm furiously typing up a Joint Commission readiness report that's due at 8 AM... but it gets done. \n\nHonestly, my bosses and the committee members probably consider me a very reliable guy because I always deliver the final product. They just don't see the absolute chaos behind the scenes. They don't see the late nights or the frantic rushing. Now, if we're talking about hobbies or stuff around the house? Sure, maybe. I bought a whole homebrewing kit a few years ago, spent a fortune on the carboys and the hops, used it exactly one time, and now it's just collecting dust in the corner of the garage. But with actual work or committee responsibilities? No, I never leave them hanging. I push through and finish them, even if the process is completely exhausting.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to motivate yourself to start tasks when you're not interested in them?\n\nPatient: Oh, man. Yes. That... honestly, that right there is the main reason I’m sitting in this chair talking to you today. It’s exactly that. When it comes to anything that takes real, sustained, sitting-down brain work, it’s like I just physically hit a wall. \n\nTake the monthly reports, for example. Every single month, I have to do these complex variance reports and budget reconciliations for the clinic. I have to pull data from our electronic health records, cross-reference it with the billing department’s spreadsheets, and write up a whole summary for the board. Or, you know, planning this massive multi-site health event we do every fall—which, by the way, is a logistical nightmare because we’re coordinating mobile vaccination units across three different counties, trying to get city permits, scheduling volunteer nurses... it’s a huge undertaking. And I know it matters. I know exactly how crucial it is for the community and for our annual funding. \n\nBut when I actually sit down at my desk, close my door, open up my laptop, and stare at that blank document or that massive Excel grid... I just can't do it. My brain just sort of rebels. It's like this invisible, impenetrable barrier drops down in front of me. So instead of just starting, I’ll find literally anything else to do. I’ll go walk the halls, I'll go chat with the front desk staff, I'll even go restock the medium-sized nitrile gloves in exam room three—which is definitely not a clinic manager's job, but I’ll do it just to avoid the heavy lifting of that mental work. I just keep putting it off and putting it off, day after day, even though I am fully aware of the deadlines looming over my head. It’s exhausting, honestly, knowing you have to do something so important and just... not being able to make yourself start.\n\nDoctor: Do you have problems controlling your emotions when you are under stress?\n\nPatient: I feel like... well, honestly, that’s pretty much the same as the frustration thing we talked about earlier. Stress and frustration, they kind of go hand-in-hand in my line of work, and my answer is pretty much the same. No, I don't have problems controlling my emotions when I'm stressed. \n\nIf anything, stress just pushes me into this sort of... crisis management mode. I mean, my baseline at the clinic is basically a constant state of moderate stress anyway, so you get used to it. Like, take last Tuesday, for example. We had an absolute perfect storm. Two of our medical assistants called out sick with the flu—or maybe it was RSV, whatever it is, it’s going around the schools right now and knocking everyone out—and then right at 9:00 AM, right when our waiting room is at maximum capacity with the morning rush, our entire electronic medical record system goes completely offline. Just... boom. Blank screens across the entire clinic. Server error. \n\nNow, the physicians, they immediately start panicking because they can't see the patient histories or put in lab orders, and the front desk staff is getting yelled at by patients who have been waiting forty-five minutes past their appointment times. The stress level in that building was palpable. You could literally cut it with a knife. And internally? Sure, my stomach is in absolute knots. I'm thinking about the revenue we're losing, the angry patient satisfaction surveys we're going to get, the absolute nightmare it's going to be for my staff to back-enter all those paper charts once the system finally comes back up. \n\nBut did I lose control of my emotions? Did I yell or freak out or shut down? Not at all. I just immediately pivoted. I grabbed the emergency downtime binders—which, thank goodness, I had actually updated myself just two months prior—and I started physically handing out paper forms to the providers. I was walking up and down the halls, checking in with the nurses, keeping my voice completely level, reassuring the patients in the waiting room. I spent two solid hours on hold with corporate IT, just sitting there listening to that awful, tinny elevator hold music, and I didn't snap at the poor tier-one tech when they finally answered the phone, even though he was asking me completely unhelpful questions about whether my computer was plugged in. \n\nSo, no. I don't lose my cool under stress. I just bottle it up, deal with the crisis step by step, and then maybe, you know, have a stiff drink and complain to my wife when I finally get home at seven o'clock. But in the moment, when the pressure is on? I'm a rock. I have to be. If the manager loses it, the whole clinic falls apart. So the emotional side of things, the stress, the frustration... that's really not the issue for me. It's just the day-to-day focus stuff that's killing me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Emotional state? I... huh. I'm really not sure how to answer that, to be honest. I mean, if you're asking if I'm depressed or angry or something when I can't start a task, then no, definitely not. Like I was saying a minute ago, my baseline is just a sort of constant, low-level hum of operational stress, but I wouldn't really call that an emotional state that stops me from working. But the interest part... well, I guess that has to play a role, but even then, it’s complicated. It’s not like I actively hate the work. I chose this field. But when I’m staring at that same budget reconciliation spreadsheet I have to do every single month... my brain just completely taps out. It’s like, if it’s a brand new problem—like that server crashing I told you about—I’m all over it. It’s new, it’s urgent, it’s a puzzle right in front of my face. But the routine stuff? So is that an emotional thing? Just being profoundly bored? I really don't know how you doctors categorize that. I just know that if a task doesn't have some kind of immediate spark or... or a massive, literal fire attached to it that I have to put out right this second, I just can't make myself engage with it. I'll sit there at my desk and think about how much I need to do it, and this heavy feeling of dread just builds and builds in my chest—so maybe dread counts as an emotion? I don't know. I'll just sit there watching the cursor blink on the screen, and I’ll tell myself, 'Okay, just type the first line of the executive summary,' and I just... don't. I'll go check the inventory on the printer toner instead, or I'll reorganize the dry-erase markers in the conference room. 

So, honestly, I couldn't really tell you if it's my emotions or just a complete lack of interest. I don't know how to untangle the two. I just know that invisible wall is there, and no matter how much I try to rationalize with myself, I can't seem to climb over it until the deadline is literally breathing down my neck.

Yes. That... honestly, that right there is the main reason I’m sitting in this chair talking to you today. It’s exactly that. When it comes to anything that takes real, sustained, sitting-down brain work, it’s like I just physically hit a wall. \n\nTake the monthly reports, for example. Every single month, I have to do these complex variance reports and budget reconciliations for the clinic. I have to pull data from our electronic health records, cross-reference it with the billing department’s spreadsheets, and write up a whole summary for the board. Or, you know, planning this massive multi-site health event we do every fall—which, by the way, is a logistical nightmare because we’re coordinating mobile vaccination units across three different counties, trying to get city permits, scheduling volunteer nurses... it’s a huge undertaking. And I know it matters. I know exactly how crucial it is for the community and for our annual funding. \n\nBut when I actually sit down at my desk, close my door, open up my laptop, and stare at that blank document or that massive Excel grid... I just can't do it. My brain just sort of rebels. It's like this invisible, impenetrable barrier drops down in front of me. So instead of just starting, I’ll find literally anything else to do. I’ll go walk the halls, I'll go chat with the front desk staff, I'll even go restock the medium-sized nitrile gloves in exam room three—which is definitely not a clinic manager's job, but I’ll do it just to avoid the heavy lifting of that mental work. I just keep putting it off and putting it off, day after day, even though I am fully aware of the deadlines looming over my head. It’s exhausting, honestly, knowing you have to do something so important and just... not being able to make yourself start.\n\nDoctor: Do you have problems controlling your emotions when you are under stress?\n\nPatient: I feel like... well, honestly, that’s pretty much the same as the frustration thing we talked about earlier. Stress and frustration, they kind of go hand-in-hand in my line of work, and my answer is pretty much the same. No, I don't have problems controlling my emotions when I'm stressed. \n\nIf anything, stress just pushes me into this sort of... crisis management mode. I mean, my baseline at the clinic is basically a constant state of moderate stress anyway, so you get used to it. Like, take last Tuesday, for example. We had an absolute perfect storm. Two of our medical assistants called out sick with the flu—or maybe it was RSV, whatever it is, it’s going around the schools right now and knocking everyone out—and then right at 9:00 AM, right when our waiting room is at maximum capacity with the morning rush, our entire electronic medical record system goes completely offline. Just... boom. Blank screens across the entire clinic. Server error. \n\nNow, the physicians, they immediately start panicking because they can't see the patient histories or put in lab orders, and the front desk staff is getting yelled at by patients who have been waiting forty-five minutes past their appointment times. The stress level in that building was palpable. You could literally cut it with a knife. And internally? Sure, my stomach is in absolute knots. I'm thinking about the revenue we're losing, the angry patient satisfaction surveys we're going to get, the absolute nightmare it's going to be for my staff to back-enter all those paper charts once the system finally comes back up. \n\nBut did I lose control of my emotions? Did I yell or freak out or shut down? Not at all. I just immediately pivoted. I grabbed the emergency downtime binders—which, thank goodness, I had actually updated myself just two months prior—and I started physically handing out paper forms to the providers. I was walking up and down the halls, checking in with the nurses, keeping my voice completely level, reassuring the patients in the waiting room. I spent two solid hours on hold with corporate IT, just sitting there listening to that awful, tinny elevator hold music, and I didn't snap at the poor tier-one tech when they finally answered the phone, even though he was asking me completely unhelpful questions about whether my computer was plugged in. \n\nSo, no. I don't lose my cool under stress. I just bottle it up, deal with the crisis step by step, and then maybe, you know, have a stiff drink and complain to my wife when I finally get home at seven o'clock. But in the moment, when the pressure is on? I'm a rock. I have to be. If the manager loses it, the whole clinic falls apart. So the emotional side of things, the stress, the frustration... that's really not the issue for me. It's just Tuesday in healthcare, you know? \n\nBut the interest part... well, I guess that has to play a role, but even then, it’s complicated. It’s not like I actively hate the work. I chose this field. I spent a long time getting my associate's degree and working my way up the ladder to become a clinic manager. I actually care about our outcomes. But when I’m staring at that same budget reconciliation spreadsheet I have to do every single month... my brain just completely taps out. It’s like, if it’s a brand new problem—like that server crashing I told you about—I’m all over it. It’s new, it’s urgent, it’s a puzzle right in front of my face. But the routine stuff? The long-term projects? The interest level drops to absolutely zero. \n\nSo is that an emotional thing? Just being profoundly bored? I really don't know how you doctors categorize that. I just know that if a task doesn't have some kind of immediate spark or... or a massive, literal fire attached to it that I have to put out right this second, I just can't make myself engage with it. I'll sit there at my desk and think about how much I need to do it, and this heavy feeling of dread just builds and builds in my chest—so maybe dread counts as an emotion? I don't know. I'll go check the inventory on the printer toner instead, or I'll reorganize the dry-erase markers in the conference room. \n\nSo, honestly, I couldn't really tell you if it's my emotions or just a complete lack of interest. I don't know how to untangle the two. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Diagnosed? No, never officially diagnosed. You know how it was growing up in the eighties, they didn't really screen for that sort of thing unless a kid was literally bouncing off the walls and disrupting the whole class, which wasn't me. I was quiet. 

But suspected? Well... looking back now, I mean, the signs were practically written in neon. Even way back in elementary school, the parent-teacher conferences were exactly the same script every single year. My teachers—I remember Mrs. Gable in third grade specifically—would sit down with my parents and tell them I was a bright kid, that I had all this potential and understood the material, but that I was just always, always daydreaming. I'd be staring out the window at the playground or watching a bug on the windowsill instead of looking at the chalkboard. 

And my organization was just a complete disaster. I was constantly losing worksheets. I'd shove them in my desk and they'd just get crumpled up into these little accordions under a pile of broken crayons. And forgetting to turn in homework? That was my specialty. I could actually do the assignment, but getting it from my hand to the teacher's tray the next morning was somehow impossible. It actually got to the point where my mother had to completely take over. Every single night, without fail, she had to physically open my backpack and check it. She'd sit there at the kitchen table, pull out all my folders, find the homework, and make sure it was ready to go. Because if she didn't check that backpack every night, if she happened to be busy or forgot, nothing was getting turned in. 

So, no doctor ever gave me a piece of paper with a diagnosis on it back then, but the pattern... I mean, it’s been there my whole life, right from the very start. It's just that now, instead of my mom checking my backpack for math worksheets, it's me staring at a clinic budget trying to figure out why my brain won't just let me do the work.
