From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.0, F43.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Hi, doctor. Thanks for seeing me. Yeah, so, what brings me in... well, it’s been building up for a while now. I’m a medical services manager, so I oversee operations for a few different clinics, which means there’s always a lot of moving parts. I'm usually fine when I'm on my feet, you know, putting out fires and talking to the nursing staff or dealing with patient complaints. \n\nBut the main reason I’m here is that anything that takes real, sitting-down brain work... I just feel like I hit a wall. Like, a literal brick wall. For instance, I have to do these detailed monthly reports on our budget variances, or right now I'm supposed to be planning this big multi-site community health event, and I just keep putting it off. I keep pushing it to the bottom of the pile, even when I know it really, really matters. \n\nI know the deadlines are looming, I know my directors need those numbers, but the thought of just sitting at my desk and focusing on that kind of heavy mental task... my brain just completely rejects it. It’s incredibly frustrating because I’ve worked really hard to get to this level in my career, especially having started out with just my associate's degree and having to prove myself over the years, and now I just can't seem to force myself to do the most important parts of my job. I'll find a hundred other little things to do instead of just sitting down and doing the one big thing I actually need to do. It's just getting to be too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about doing those detailed reports or planning the health event?\n\nPatient: Anxious? No, I wouldn't say it's anxiety. I mean, I know what anxiety feels like, my sister has terrible anxiety, and she gets the racing heart and the sweating and all that, and it's not like that for me at all. It's more just... a complete lack of ability to start. \n\nLike, when I think about the budget variance report, which is due on the fifth of every month, by the way, and requires me to pull data from three different scheduling systems and cross-reference it with our billing software... it's not that I'm scared of the report. I know how to do it. I've done it a dozen times. But when I look at the icon on my desktop, or when I open the Excel file, it's just this overwhelming feeling of... ugh. Just, ugh. My brain just slides right off it. \n\nI don't sit there worrying about whether I'll do a bad job or if the directors will be mad at me—well, I mean, I do worry about them being mad if it's late, but the actual task itself doesn't make me nervous. It's just so incredibly tedious and requires so much sustained attention, and I just find myself thinking, \"Oh, I should really check if the maintenance guy fixed that leaky faucet in clinic B,\" or \"I need to reply to that email from the vendor about the new gloves.\" And before I know it, two hours have passed, and I haven't typed a single number into the spreadsheet. So, no, not anxious. Just... completely unable to make myself do it, if that makes sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad or depressed when you're unable to start or complete these tasks?\n\nPatient: Sad or depressed? No, I really wouldn't use those words. I mean, sure, I get frustrated, like I was saying before, because I know I should just be able to do it, you know? But it's not a sadness. It's not like I'm sitting at my desk crying over the budget report or feeling hopeless about my life. I actually really like my life, and I like my job—most of it, anyway. \n\nI'm not walking around feeling down in the dumps or, I don't know, empty or anything like that. It's more of an annoyance with myself. Like, I'll be sitting there, staring at the screen, and I'll think, \"Come on, man, just do the thing.\" But my brain is just stubbornly refusing. And then I get mad at myself for wasting time, especially when I know I'm perfectly capable of doing the work. I mean, I didn't get to be a manager by being stupid, right? I started out at the front desk with just my associate's degree and worked my way up, so I know how to work hard. \n\nBut this specific kind of work... it just feels impossible sometimes. So, no, not depressed. Just... really, really irritated that I can't seem to make my brain cooperate when it comes to the boring, sit-down stuff. It's like trying to drive a car with the parking brake on, you know? You're pressing the gas, but you're just not going anywhere, and you can smell the brakes burning, and it's just a giant hassle. But it doesn't make me sad. Just annoyed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find your mind wandering when you're in the middle of a task?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. All the time. It's actually a huge problem for me, especially in meetings. And I have to sit through a lot of meetings in my role, as you can probably imagine. Like, just last Tuesday, we had this big cross-clinic leadership meeting. We were going over the quarterly budget updates, which, again, is important stuff that I really need to know for my own reporting. \n\nSo I'm sitting there, and I'm really trying to listen to the finance director—he's this guy named Tom, nice guy, but he has this very monotone voice, which doesn't help. But anyway, I'm trying to focus on what he's saying about the Q3 projections. And then, someone's phone vibrates on the table. It wasn't even a ring, just that little *bzz bzz*, and instantly, my brain is just... gone. I'm looking at the phone, wondering whose it is, wondering if it's an emergency at one of the clinics. And then while I'm thinking about that, I hear some nurses talking out in the hallway. I can't quite make out what they're saying, but I can hear the cadence of it, and I start trying to figure out who's talking and what they're discussing. \n\nOr, even worse, it's not even an outside noise. It's my own brain. Like, in that same meeting, after the phone thing, I suddenly remembered that I forgot to email the guys in my weekend softball league about the schedule change for Sunday. And once that thought entered my head, it was like a train leaving the station. I'm sitting there, physically in the room, looking right at Tom, but mentally I'm drafting the email to the team, thinking about who can play first base since Dave is out of town, and wondering if we have enough softballs in the equipment bag. \n\nAnd then, suddenly, I snap back to reality, and I realize Tom has moved on to the next slide, and I have absolutely no idea what he just said for the last three or four minutes. I completely missed the part about the new supply chain vendor, which I really needed to know. It's so frustrating because I'm not trying to ignore him, and I'm not trying to be disrespectful. My mind just... gets hijacked by whatever little thing pops up, whether it's a sound in the room or just a random thought in my head. And it takes me a minute to even realize that I've drifted off. So, yeah, wandering mind? Definitely. It's a constant battle.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Act on impulse? No, not really. I mean, I'm not one of those guys who just up and buys a sports car on a Tuesday or, you know, decides to go skydiving on a whim. I'm actually pretty predictable in that regard. My wife always jokes that I'm a creature of habit. I eat the same turkey sandwich for lunch almost every day—Boar's Head turkey, provolone, a little bit of mayo on wheat bread, usually from that deli down the street from the main clinic. \n\nBut as far as making impulsive decisions at work or in life, no, I don't really do that. I mean, in my job, you can't really afford to be impulsive, right? As a manager, especially in healthcare, everything has a protocol. If a patient complains or if there's a staffing shortage because three nurses called out sick with the flu—which actually happened last winter, it was a nightmare, we had to pull staff from two other locations just to keep the urgent care side open—you have to follow the procedures. You can't just fly off the handle or make snap decisions without thinking about the downstream effects. \n\nAnd even financially, like I said, I'm dealing with budgets and variance reports. I don't just randomly approve a purchase order for a new EKG machine without running it up the flagpole first. I'm actually pretty careful about that stuff. If anything, I might overthink things sometimes, or just put them off, like we talked about earlier. But I don't just blurt things out in meetings or make wild choices without thinking. I'm pretty grounded, honestly. It's just the focusing and the starting that trip me up, not doing things too fast or without thinking. So, yeah, no real issues with impulsivity.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Restless or fidgety? Hmm. You know, not really. I mean, I'm not bouncing off the walls or anything. I don't sit there shaking my leg up and down like a jackhammer, which I know some people do. My brother-in-law, for instance, he cannot sit still at a family dinner to save his life. He's always tapping the table or jiggling his knee, and it honestly drives my sister crazy. But no, I don't really do that.\n\nI mean, sure, if I'm stuck in one of those three-hour strategic planning sessions—and we have those every quarter, usually in this conference room that has the most uncomfortable chairs known to man, they're those stiff mesh ones that look ergonomic but really just dig into your lower back—I'll shift around in my seat. I'll cross my legs, uncross them, maybe stretch my back a little bit. But I think anyone would do that, right? You sit in a bad chair for three hours listening to someone talk about synergy and patient retention metrics, you're going to want to move around a little. \n\nBut I don't feel this, like, internal need to move. I'm not crawling out of my skin if I have to sit still. When I'm at my desk trying to force myself to look at those variance reports, I'm sitting perfectly still. I'm just staring at the monitor, feeling annoyed with myself, like I said before. But my body isn't restless. I'm not pacing the floor of my office or spinning in my chair or anything like that. \n\nEven when I'm at home, you know, watching TV in the evening. We usually watch a couple of episodes of whatever show we're currently binging—right now it's that new crime drama on streaming, the one set in Chicago, it's pretty good, although the medical scenes are always so inaccurate it makes me laugh. But anyway, I can just sit on the couch for two hours straight, no problem. I might get up to grab a glass of water or let the dog out—we have a golden retriever, Buster, he's great but he always wants to go out right in the middle of an episode—but I'm not fidgeting the whole time. So, yeah, I'd say physically, I'm pretty calm. It's just my brain that won't sit still, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to sustain your attention on tasks that require continuous mental effort?\n\nPatient: Oh, man. Yes. Honestly... that right there is the main reason I’m here talking to you today. It’s exactly that. Anything that takes real, continuous, sitting-down brain work—it just feels like I hit a massive brick wall. \n\nIf I’m walking around the clinic, putting out fires, dealing with a scheduling mix-up or a patient complaint, I’m totally fine. But the heavy mental lifting? It’s a completely different story. Like those monthly reports I mentioned earlier. I have to pull data from three different scheduling systems because we still haven’t unified our software—which, by the way, is a whole other headache we’ve been arguing with corporate about for two years. They keep promising this new system rollout, but it just keeps getting delayed, so we're stuck doing it the hard way. Anyway, I have to sit there, compile all this raw data, and write a detailed narrative explaining, you know, why overtime was up fifteen percent in urgent care last month. \n\nOr another huge one is planning our multi-site health events. We do this big community outreach thing every fall—free flu shots, blood pressure screenings, diabetes education—and it requires bringing in staff from four different clinics to run the tents. It’s incredibly important stuff. I know it matters, the community relies on it, and my bosses expect it to be flawless. But when I sit down at my desk to actually do the logistical planning for it, to just focus and grind out the permit applications or the volunteer schedules... my brain just refuses to engage. \n\nI will do literally anything else to avoid that sustained mental effort. I’ll reorganize my email folders, I’ll go out to the nurses' station and restock the printer paper, I’ll end up chatting with the front desk receptionist for twenty minutes about her kid’s travel soccer team. I just keep putting the big tasks off and putting them off, even when I know how critical they are. It’s not that I don’t know how to do the work—I’ve been managing these clinics for years—but the actual act of sitting there and forcing my brain to stay on track for that long... it's like trying to push a boulder up a hill. I just avoid it until the panic of the deadline finally kicks in, and it's just exhausting living like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your time and prioritizing tasks?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. Organizing and prioritizing is probably where things fall apart the most. \n\nIt’s like, when I’m juggling all my regular work projects—the staffing, the variance reports, all that day-to-day clinic stuff—and then on top of that, I’m helping out with this memorial committee stuff for my uncle's old VFW post, plus those big community outreach events I just mentioned... it’s just too many moving parts. I’ll write something down on a yellow legal pad during a morning meeting, and then later I’ll jot a reminder on a Post-it note and stick it to my steering wheel so I don't forget it on the drive home, and then I’ll also have a random, half-finished list going in the Notes app on my iPhone. \n\nMy wife actually bought me one of those expensive leather-bound planners for Christmas two years ago—really nice quality, monogrammed and everything—thinking it would help me consolidate all these floating pieces of paper. I think I used it for about three days before I buried it under a pile of mail on the kitchen counter, and now I don't even know where it is. \n\nBut anyway, because everything is scattered across all these different places, when I actually sit down and try to plan my day, I just draw a blank. Which I absolutely didn't, I had the carbon copy of the purchase order right in front of me. \n\nSo, was I frustrated? Oh, incredibly. I just took a deep breath, calmly explained that I was looking at the PO with the correct item number, and told him he needed to courier the right supplies over from their regional warehouse within the hour. \n\nI actually pride myself on that, honestly. Being the calm one in the storm. You kind of have to be when you're managing a clinic. So, yeah, wandering mind? Definitely. It's a constant battle.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without fully thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Act on impulse? No, not really. I mean, I'm not one of those guys who just up and buys a sports car on a Tuesday or, you know, decides to go skydiving on a whim. I'm actually pretty predictable in that regard. My wife always jokes that I'm a creature of habit. I eat the same turkey sandwich for lunch almost every day—Boar's Head turkey, provolone, a little bit of mayo on wheat bread, usually from that deli down the street from the main clinic. \n\nBut as far as making impulsive decisions at work or in life, no, I don't really do that. I mean, in my job, you can't really afford to be impulsive, right? As a manager, especially in healthcare, everything has a protocol. If a patient complains or if there's a staffing shortage because three nurses called out sick with the flu—which actually happened last winter, it was a nightmare, we had to pull staff from two other locations just to keep the urgent care side open—you have to follow the procedures. You can't just fly off the handle or make snap decisions without thinking about the downstream effects. \n\nAnd even financially, like I said, I'm dealing with budgets and variance reports. I don't just randomly approve a purchase order for a new EKG machine without running it up the flagpole first. I'm actually pretty careful about that stuff. If anything, I might overthink things sometimes, or just put them off, like we talked about earlier. But I don't just blurt things out in meetings or make wild choices without thinking. I'm pretty grounded, honestly. It's just the focusing and the starting that trip me up, not doing things too fast or without thinking. So, yeah, no real issues with impulsivity.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Restless or fidgety? Hmm. You know, not really. I mean, I'm not bouncing off the walls or anything. I don't sit there shaking my leg up and down like a jackhammer, which I know some people do. My brother-in-law, for instance, he cannot sit still at a family dinner to save his life. He's always tapping the table or jiggling his knee, and it honestly drives my sister crazy. But no, I don't really do that.\n\nI mean, sure, if I'm stuck in one of those three-hour strategic planning sessions—and we have those every quarter, usually in this conference room that has the most uncomfortable chairs known to man, they're those stiff mesh ones that look ergonomic but really just dig into your lower back—I'll shift around in my seat. I'll cross my legs, uncross them, maybe stretch my back a little bit. But I think anyone would do that, right? You sit in a bad chair for three hours listening to someone talk about synergy and patient retention metrics, you're going to want to move around a little. \n\nBut I don't feel this, like, internal need to move. I'm not crawling out of my skin if I have to sit still. When I'm at my desk trying to force myself to look at those variance reports, I'm sitting perfectly still. I'm just staring at the monitor, feeling annoyed with myself, like I said before. But my body isn't restless. I'm not pacing the floor of my office or spinning in my chair or anything like that. \n\nEven when I'm at home, you know, watching TV in the evening. We usually watch a couple of episodes of whatever show we're currently binging—right now it's that new crime drama on streaming, the one set in Chicago, it's pretty good, although the medical scenes are always so inaccurate it makes me laugh. But anyway, I can just sit on the couch for two hours straight, no problem. I might get up to grab a glass of water or let the dog out—we have a golden retriever, Buster, he's great but he always wants to go out right in the middle of an episode—but I'm not fidgeting the whole time. So, yeah, I'd say physically, I'm pretty calm. It's just my brain that won't sit still, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to sustain your attention on tasks that require continuous mental effort?\n\nPatient: Oh, man. Yes. Honestly... that right there is the main reason I’m here talking to you today. It’s exactly that. Anything that takes real, continuous, sitting-down brain work—it just feels like I hit a massive brick wall. \n\nIf I’m walking around the clinic, putting out fires, dealing with a scheduling mix-up or a patient complaint, I’m totally fine. But the heavy mental lifting? It’s a completely different story. Like those monthly reports I mentioned earlier. I have to pull data from three different scheduling systems because we still haven’t unified our software—which, by the way, is a whole other headache we’ve been arguing with corporate about for two years. They keep promising this new system rollout, but it just keeps getting delayed, so we're stuck doing it the hard way. Anyway, I have to sit there, compile all this raw data, and write a detailed narrative explaining, you know, why overtime was up fifteen percent in urgent care last month. \n\nOr another huge one is planning our multi-site health events. We do this big community outreach thing every fall—free flu shots, blood pressure screenings, diabetes education—and it requires bringing in staff from four different clinics to run the tents. It’s incredibly important stuff. I know it matters, the community relies on it, and my bosses expect it to be flawless. But when I sit down at my desk to actually do the logistical planning for it, to just focus and grind out the permit applications or the volunteer schedules... my brain just refuses to engage. \n\nI will do literally anything else to avoid that sustained mental effort. I’ll reorganize my email folders, I’ll go out to the nurses' station and restock the printer paper, I’ll end up chatting with the front desk receptionist for twenty minutes about her kid’s travel soccer team. I just keep putting the big tasks off and putting them off, even when I know how critical they are. It’s not that I don’t know how to do the work—I’ve been managing these clinics for years—but the actual act of sitting there and forcing my brain to stay on track for that long... it's like trying to push a boulder up a hill. I just avoid it until the panic of the deadline finally kicks in, and it's just exhausting living like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble organizing your time and prioritizing tasks?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. That is... yeah, that's a huge struggle for me. Organizing and prioritizing is probably where things fall apart the most. \n\nIt’s like, when I’m juggling all my regular work projects—the staffing, the variance reports, all that day-to-day clinic stuff—and then on top of that, I’m helping out with this memorial committee stuff for my uncle's old VFW post, plus those big community outreach events I just mentioned... it’s just too many moving parts. And my system for keeping track of it all is, well, it's not really a system at all. I’ll literally have notes in three different places. I’ll write something down on a yellow legal pad during a morning meeting, and then later I’ll jot a reminder on a Post-it note and stick it to my steering wheel so I don't forget it on the drive home, and then I’ll also have a random, half-finished list going in the Notes app on my iPhone. \n\nMy wife actually bought me one of those expensive leather-bound planners for Christmas two years ago—really nice quality, monogrammed and everything—thinking it would help me consolidate all these floating pieces of paper. I think I used it for about three days before I buried it under a pile of mail on the kitchen counter, and now I don't even know where it is. \n\nBut anyway, because everything is scattered across all these different places, when I actually sit down and try to plan my day, I just draw a blank. I look at all of it and I can’t seem to figure out what needs to happen first. Is it more important to call the caterer for the memorial dinner, or do I need to finalize the permit for the flu clinic first? I can't figure out the logical order of operations. \n\nSo what happens is, I just kind of spin my wheels. I'll bounce back and forth, maybe do a tiny piece of one thing, then get distracted by an email, and I never actually set a real priority. I always manage to pull it off somehow, but the rushing at the end is just exhausting. It feels like I'm always one step away from dropping a major ball because I can't just organize a simple timeline.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble managing your emotions when faced with frustrating situations?\n\nPatient: Managing my emotions? Hmm. No, I wouldn't say that. I mean... I definitely get frustrated, don't get me wrong. Anyone working in healthcare administration right now is frustrated on a daily basis. Being the calm one in the storm. You kind of have to be when you're managing a clinic. If the doctors are stressed and the nurses are stressed, the manager can't be flying off the handle or breaking down. So no, I don't really struggle with my emotions. I keep things pretty steady. She'd sit there at the kitchen table, pull out all my folders, find the homework, and make sure it was ready to go.
